### Report rank 8: Pharma Nord — Pharma Nord Bio-Pycnogenol 40 mg tablets (30, 60 or 150)

- **Verifier verdict:** acceptable — Genuine Pycnogenol in the 40 mg unit that makes the 80 mg trial dose exact, but it only works as the Pycnogenol half of a rebuilt Prelox (paired with a certified arginine or citrulline), it carries no certification, and its label could not be re-read in this run.
- **Product as verified:** Pharma Nord — Pharma Nord Bio-Pycnogenol 40 mg tablets (30, 60 or 150)
- **Product page:** https://www.pharmanord.co.uk/all-products/heart-health-circulation/pycnogenol-supplements
- **Where sold:** UK and EU (pharmanord.co.uk, pharmanord.eu, Dutch shop), per discovery and not re-verified. Gulf: not found by discovery, and Pharma Nord's country selector lists no GCC country (prior project research).
- **Label verified:** no. Discovery's reading of the Pharma Nord UK and EU product pages (https://www.pharmanord.co.uk/all-products/heart-health-circulation/pycnogenol-supplements; https://www.pharmanord.eu/products/bio-pycnogenol). Not re-read in this run, because this session's network policy blocks pharmanord.co.uk; prior project research did not capture this product.
- **Proprietary blend:** no
- **Directions and daily amounts:** Per discovery: 1 tablet = Pycnogenol (French maritime pine bark extract) 40 mg. Directions: '2 tablets twice daily for 1 week, then 1 tablet twice daily, or as directed by a physician'. Daily amounts: week 1 = 4 tablets = 160 mg Pycnogenol; after that, 2 tablets = 80 mg. A single active ingredient, with no blend. Excipients not captured.
- **Dose versus trials (partial):** Maintenance (2 tablets/day) = 80 mg Pycnogenol, exactly the Pycnogenol dose in the positive Prelox RCTs, which paired it with 2.8-3 g/day L-arginine aspartate (Ledda 2010, Stanislavov 2008; https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10614297/). Three tablets = 120 mg, the dose in the one small trial of Pycnogenol alone in men with ED and raised cholesterol (3 months; https://www.oatext.com/review-influence-of-combinations-of-pycnogenolr-and-l-arginine-aspartate-on-mens-virility-and-fertility.php). It is also the dose in month 3 of Stanislavov 2003, which used 40 mg tablets (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/12851125/). The week-1 dose of 160 mg is above the ED trials but below the 200 mg/day used for 8 weeks in a coronary-disease RCT (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22240497/) and for 3 months with stable liver, kidney and blood tests (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13696226/). On its own it reproduces only that very preliminary monotherapy trial. To rebuild Prelox it must be paired with about 1.6-1.7 g/day L-arginine (2.8-3 g arginine aspartate), or with a Prelox R-style 1.92 g arginine + 1.2 g citrulline.
- **Certification:** None found. Discovery found no single-ingredient Pycnogenol product in Informed Sport/Informed Choice, BSCG or USP searches. ConsumerLab says it has not tested any Pycnogenol or pine-bark product, because current assays cannot reliably tell it apart from peanut-skin extract (https://www.consumerlab.com/answers/does-pycnogenol-work/pycnogenol/). Genuine trademarked Pycnogenol is a quality positive, not a certification. No certifier database could be opened in this run. (certifier page: none; verified in the certifier's database: no; verifier marked the programme as screening for hidden drugs: no — see the challenge notes, which found PDE5-drug screening confirmed only for BSCG)
- **Manufacturer and regulatory record:** Maker: Pharma Nord ApS, Vojens, Denmark, which states it has its own GMP plant 'under the auspices of the Danish Medicines Agency' (company statement, per discovery, https://shop.pharmanord.eu/welcome/produced-in-denmark). Pycnogenol is Horphag Research's trademarked extract. Discovery reported no recall, warning letter or hidden-drug report for Pharma Nord; prior project research has no record specific to this product. VERIFICATION LIMIT, this run: the shared WebSearch call limit was reached and the session's network allows only package registries and MCP servers, so FDA, MHRA/FSA, RASFF, Danish food-agency, Health Canada and TGA records could not be searched. These limits were not routed around. Allow the hosts, or raise CLAUDE_CODE_MAX_WEB_SEARCHES_PER_SESSION, and re-run to complete the check. Status: no red flag known; check incomplete.
- **Evidence summary:** Evidence for Pycnogenol in ED comes almost entirely from the Prelox combination (3 Horphag-linked RCTs; pooled IIEF-EF +8.9 vs placebo; https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10614297/). For Pycnogenol alone, a 2018 systematic review found one small Pinus pinaster RCT (n = 21) and rated the evidence 'very preliminary' (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29633089/). In the uncontrolled Stanislavov 2003 sequence, arginine alone gave normal erections in 5% of men; adding 80 mg/day Pycnogenol raised that to 80%, and 120 mg/day to 92.5% (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/12851125/). Mechanistic support: Pycnogenol augmented endothelium-dependent vasodilation in humans (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/18037769/). The EAU groups pine bark extract with antioxidants that give only small improvements patients may not notice (https://uroweb.org/guidelines/sexual-and-reproductive-health/chapter/management-of-erectile-dysfunction).

**Safety risks**

- Usually well tolerated. Reported effects are dizziness, drowsiness, rash and stomach complaints (Examine, https://examine.com/supplements/pycnogenol/), plus irritability or lower energy (MSKCC, https://www.mskcc.org/cancer-care/integrative-medicine/herbs/pine-bark-extract).
- Antiplatelet effect: possible bleeding with blood thinners (MSKCC). 100 mg matched 500 mg aspirin against smoking-induced platelet aggregation (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/10498385/), although 27 studies reported no increased bleeding (Examine).
- May lower fasting blood glucose (Examine).
- The week-1 direction (160 mg/day) doubles the Prelox-trial Pycnogenol dose for 7 days; that is still under the 200 mg/day studied with stable safety labs (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13696226/).
- It has no proven erectile effect on its own. Once it is paired with arginine or citrulline, those products' blood-pressure cautions apply.
- Not independently certified, so identity and the absence of contaminants or hidden drugs rest on the maker's own controls.

**Interactions**

- Anticoagulants and antiplatelets (warfarin, apixaban, rivaroxaban, clopidogrel, aspirin, NSAIDs): additive antiplatelet effect and bleeding risk (MSKCC).
- Diabetes drugs (insulin, sulfonylureas, metformin): may add to glucose lowering (Examine).
- Antihypertensives: Examine lists interactions with blood-pressure-lowering drugs.
- Nitrates and PDE5 inhibitors: no interaction documented for Pycnogenol alone. When paired with arginine or citrulline (its purpose here), avoid it with nitrates and monitor blood pressure with PDE5 inhibitors (Drugs.com, https://www.drugs.com/drug-interactions/arginine-with-sildenafil-232-0-2061-0.html?professional=1).
- Thyroid drugs, SSRIs/MAOIs and sedatives: no interaction documented in the sources reviewed. The ABC monograph notes that no formal drug-interaction studies exist (per discovery, https://v9.pycnogenol.com/fileadmin/pdf/ABC_Pycnogenol_Monograph_2019_-_Consumer_Patient_Information_Sheet.pdf).

**Who should avoid it**

- Men on anticoagulants or antiplatelets, or with a bleeding disorder, unless a doctor agrees; stop before planned surgery.
- Pine allergy (ABC monograph, per discovery).
- Men on insulin or sulfonylureas who are prone to low blood sugar, unless monitored.
- Anyone who would pair it with arginine or citrulline while taking nitrates.
- Men with new, unexplained ED who have not had a cardiovascular check (AUA, https://www.auanet.org/guidelines-and-quality/guidelines/erectile-dysfunction-(ed)-guideline).

**Stop signals**

- Unusual bruising, nosebleeds, or blood in urine or stool.
- Persistent dizziness, headache or stomach upset.
- Rash or other allergic reaction.
- Low-blood-sugar symptoms if taking diabetes medicines.
- No gain of at least 4 IIEF erectile-domain points (2 if ED is mild) after 8-12 weeks on the full Pycnogenol + arginine regimen (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21855209/).

**Challenge reviews**

- *certification-label lens — confirmed; downgrade to: none*
  - Audit basis: raw page captures from this workflow (7-8 Nov 2026), not the verifier's summary; live re-opening was blocked today (network policy, spent WebSearch budget, Firecrawl out of credits until 2026-11-09 05:26 UTC).
  - No certifier lists any single-ingredient Pycnogenol in the captured data: no pine-bark product in the NSF directory of 8 Nov 2026, and ConsumerLab says it has not tested pine bark (https://www.consumerlab.com/answers/does-pycnogenol-work/pycnogenol/).
  - Label (40 mg Pycnogenol per tablet; 2 tablets twice daily for one week, then 1 tablet twice daily; packs of 30, 60 and 150) is supported only by a search-engine summary of Pharma Nord's UK page; excipients were not captured.

- *safety-evidence lens — corrected; downgrade to: none*
  - Missed caution: monographs based on Natural Medicines (WebMD) list an immunosuppressant interaction and advise avoiding Pycnogenol in autoimmune disease (theoretical immune stimulation). From my own knowledge; not re-opened this pass.
  - Identity risk: ConsumerLab will not test pine-bark products because its assay can be fooled by cheaper peanut-skin extract. An adulterated product would contain undeclared peanut material, which matters for men with a peanut allergy (inference). Buying trademarked Pycnogenol through an authorised channel reduces this risk, but with no certification it remains.
  - The week-1 direction (160 mg/day) is double the Pycnogenol dose of the Prelox trials, with no ED rationale. For this purpose, start at 2 tablets a day (80 mg) alongside a certified arginine.
  - Evidence for Pycnogenol alone is one 21-man trial. The benefit claims rest on the Horphag-linked combination trials.

### Report rank 9: Life Extension — Life Extension Pycnogenol 100 mg (60 vegetarian capsules, item 01637)

- **Verifier verdict:** acceptable — Lets the user keep his current brand while swapping LE Prelox's hidden blend for a disclosed 100 mg Pycnogenol dose, from a maker whose only documented FDA action is a 2017 marketing-claims letter and which sells in Gulf pharmacies; but it has no product-level certification and needs a separate arginine or citrulline.
- **Product as verified:** Life Extension — Life Extension Pycnogenol 100 mg (60 vegetarian capsules, item 01637)
- **Product page:** https://www.lifeextension.com/vitamins-supplements/item01637
- **Where sold:** US (lifeextension.com, Vitacost, Swanson); Gulf: Nahdi UAE and Saudi Arabia; EU: Redcare France and Smeets & Graas. All per discovery and not re-verified. Unlike LE Prelox, which is licence-locked to the US (https://www.lifeextension.com/vitamins-supplements/item01373/prelox-enhanced-sex), this product is sold abroad.
- **Label verified:** no. Discovery's reading of lifeextension.com item 01637, Vitacost and NIH DSLD label PDF 182070 (https://api.ods.od.nih.gov/dsld/s3/pdf/182070.pdf). Not re-read in this run: the session network blocks these hosts, and the proxy log shows dsld.od.nih.gov refused.
- **Proprietary blend:** no
- **Directions and daily amounts:** Per discovery: serving 1 vegetarian capsule = Pycnogenol (dried French maritime pine bark extract, standardized to 65% procyanidins) 100 mg. The DSLD label also lists vitamin C (as ascorbyl palmitate) 4 mg. 60 capsules. Other ingredients vary by label version: either rice flour, vegetable cellulose and silica, or microcrystalline cellulose, vegetable cellulose, stearic acid and silica. Directions were not captured; at 1 capsule a day the daily amount is 100 mg Pycnogenol + 4 mg vitamin C. Fully disclosed, with no blend.
- **Dose versus trials (partial):** 100 mg/day, against 80 mg/day Pycnogenol in the positive Prelox RCTs (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10614297/) and 120 mg/day in the single small Pycnogenol-alone trial (https://www.oatext.com/review-influence-of-combinations-of-pycnogenolr-and-l-arginine-aspartate-on-mens-virility-and-fertility.php). It is about 2.5 times the roughly 40 mg/day that LE Prelox would supply at its 2-tablet maintenance dose, if LE follows Horphag's 20 mg-per-tablet formulation (LE does not disclose this). On its own it reproduces no robust ED trial. To rebuild the Prelox regimen with known amounts, pair it with about 1.6-1.7 g/day L-arginine, or with arginine + citrulline.
- **Certification:** None at product level. Life Extension offers certificates of analysis for every product on request and cites NSF GMP facility registration (https://www.lifeextension.com/about/life-extension-research/safety). GMP registration covers the facility, not this product, and involves no drug screening. No certifier database could be opened in this run. (certifier page: none; verified in the certifier's database: no; verifier marked the programme as screening for hidden drugs: no — see the challenge notes, which found PDE5-drug screening confirmed only for BSCG)
- **Manufacturer and regulatory record:** Maker: Life Extension (Life Extension Foundation Buyers Club, Inc., Fort Lauderdale, Florida; founded 1980, per prior research). FDA Warning Letter 17-07 (1 Feb 2017) cited disease claims in its online health protocols, including an erectile-dysfunction protocol, across more than 400 products. That was a marketing-claims action, not adulteration or manufacturing (https://www.fda.gov/inspections-compliance-enforcement-and-criminal-investigations/warning-letters/life-extension-foundation-buyers-club-inc-500619-02012017). - **Evidence summary:** Pycnogenol's ED evidence comes from the Prelox combination with arginine (3 small Horphag-linked RCTs; pooled IIEF-EF +8.9 vs placebo; https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10614297/). For Pycnogenol alone there is one small RCT (n = 21), rated 'very preliminary' in a 2018 systematic review (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29633089/). The uncontrolled Stanislavov 2003 sequence suggests Pycnogenol adds to arginine (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/12851125/). Pycnogenol augmented endothelium-dependent vasodilation in humans (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/18037769/). The EAU places pine bark extract among antioxidants with only small effects (https://uroweb.org/guidelines/sexual-and-reproductive-health/chapter/management-of-erectile-dysfunction). The LE product itself has no trial.

**Safety risks**

- Usually well tolerated. Reported effects are dizziness, drowsiness, rash and stomach complaints (Examine, https://examine.com/supplements/pycnogenol/), plus irritability or lower energy (MSKCC, https://www.mskcc.org/cancer-care/integrative-medicine/herbs/pine-bark-extract).
- Antiplatelet effect: possible bleeding with blood thinners (MSKCC; 100 mg matched 500 mg aspirin against smoking-induced aggregation, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/10498385/), though 27 studies reported no increased bleeding (Examine).
- May lower fasting blood glucose (Examine).
- It has no proven erectile effect on its own. Once it is paired with arginine or citrulline, those products' blood-pressure cautions apply.
- No product-level certification. Quality assurance rests on Life Extension's COAs (available on request) and facility GMP registration.

**Interactions**

- Anticoagulants and antiplatelets (warfarin, apixaban, rivaroxaban, clopidogrel, aspirin, NSAIDs): additive antiplatelet effect and bleeding risk (MSKCC).

- Antihypertensives: Examine lists interactions with blood-pressure-lowering drugs.
- Nitrates and PDE5 inhibitors: no interaction documented for Pycnogenol alone. When paired with arginine or citrulline, avoid it with nitrates and monitor blood pressure with PDE5 inhibitors (Drugs.com, https://www.drugs.com/drug-interactions/arginine-with-sildenafil-232-0-2061-0.html?professional=1).
- Thyroid drugs, SSRIs/MAOIs and sedatives: no interaction documented in the sources reviewed.

**Who should avoid it**

- Men on anticoagulants or antiplatelets, or with a bleeding disorder, unless a doctor agrees; stop before planned surgery.
- Pine allergy.
- Men on insulin or sulfonylureas who are prone to low blood sugar, unless monitored.
- Anyone who would pair it with arginine or citrulline while taking nitrates.
- Men with new, unexplained ED who have not had a cardiovascular check (AUA, https://www.auanet.org/guidelines-and-quality/guidelines/erectile-dysfunction-(ed)-guideline).

**Stop signals**

- Unusual bruising, nosebleeds, or blood in urine or stool.
- Persistent dizziness, headache or stomach upset.
- Rash or other allergic reaction.
- Low-blood-sugar symptoms if taking diabetes medicines.
- No gain of at least 4 IIEF erectile-domain points (2 if ED is mild) after 8-12 weeks on the full Pycnogenol + arginine regimen (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21855209/).

### Report rank 10: Solgar — Solgar Pycnogenol 100 mg vegetable capsules (30)

- **Verifier verdict:** acceptable — Genuine Pycnogenol that is the easiest to find in UAE and Saudi pharmacies, and one capsule is close to the trial dose; but it is uncertified, its directions conflict (up to 200 mg/day), and Solgar's regulatory record was not checked, so use it only as a fallback Pycnogenol half alongside a certified arginine or citrulline.
- **Product as verified:** Solgar — Solgar Pycnogenol 100 mg vegetable capsules (30)
- **Product page:** https://lifepharmacy.com/product/solgar-pycnogenol-100-mg-vegetable-capsules-30-s
- **Where sold:** Gulf: Life Pharmacy UAE and Saudi Arabia, and Sporter Saudi Arabia. Also UK (John Bell & Croyden, Cult Beauty), EU (Medino, Greek pharmacies, Decathlon Spain) and US (Vitacost). All of this is per discovery and not re-verified; prior project research obtained no Gulf pharmacy listings, so Gulf availability rests on discovery alone.
- **Label verified:** no. Discovery's reading of the Life Pharmacy (UAE and KSA), Sporter, Solgar (https://www.solgar.com/?p=411) and Vitacost listings. Not re-read in this run because of the session network block; prior project research holds no Solgar label.
- **Proprietary blend:** no
- **Directions and daily amounts:** Per discovery: 1 vegetable capsule = Pycnogenol (French maritime pine bark extract, Pinus pinaster) 100 mg; 30 capsules. Directions conflict: Solgar's site says 1 capsule twice daily at mealtimes (200 mg/day), while some retailers say 1-2 daily (100-200 mg/day). Inactive ingredients also differ between listings (one lists microcrystalline cellulose, vegetable cellulose and vegetable magnesium stearate), so read the bottle in hand. For the Prelox-trial Pycnogenol dose, 1 capsule a day (100 mg) is enough. A single active ingredient, with no blend.
- **Dose versus trials (partial):** 1 capsule = 100 mg/day. That is 25% above the 80 mg/day in the positive Prelox RCTs, which added 2.8-3 g arginine aspartate (Ledda 2010, Stanislavov 2008; https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10614297/). It is just below the 120 mg/day in the one small Pycnogenol-alone ED trial (https://www.oatext.com/review-influence-of-combinations-of-pycnogenolr-and-l-arginine-aspartate-on-mens-virility-and-fertility.php), and inside the 60-120 mg/day range across the Prelox studies. Solgar's twice-daily direction gives 200 mg/day, 2.5 times the Prelox-trial dose, although 200 mg/day has been studied for 8 weeks (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22240497/) and for 3 months with stable labs (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13696226/). On its own it reproduces no robust ED trial; pair it with about 1.6-1.7 g/day L-arginine, or with arginine + citrulline.
- **Certification:** None found. Discovery found no certifier listing, and ConsumerLab has not tested pine-bark products (https://www.consumerlab.com/answers/does-pycnogenol-work/pycnogenol/). The product uses the Pycnogenol trademark (per discovery). No certifier database could be opened in this run. (certifier page: none; verified in the certifier's database: no; verifier marked the programme as screening for hidden drugs: no — see the challenge notes, which found PDE5-drug screening confirmed only for BSCG)
- **Manufacturer and regulatory record:** Brand: Solgar (US supplement brand). Neither prior project research nor discovery recorded any Solgar regulatory history. VERIFICATION LIMIT, this run: the shared WebSearch call limit was reached and the session's network allows only package registries and MCP servers, so FDA warning letters and recalls, FDA tainted-product notifications, Health Canada, TGA and MHRA could not be searched. These limits were not routed around. Allow the hosts, or raise CLAUDE_CODE_MAX_WEB_SEARCHES_PER_SESSION, and re-run. Status: unknown. No red flag is known, but no clean record has been established either, so check before relying on it.
- **Evidence summary:** Pycnogenol's ED evidence comes from the Prelox combination with arginine (3 small Horphag-linked RCTs; pooled IIEF-EF +8.9 vs placebo; https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10614297/). For Pycnogenol alone there is one small RCT (n = 21), which a 2018 systematic review rated 'very preliminary' (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29633089/). The uncontrolled Stanislavov 2003 sequence suggests Pycnogenol adds to arginine (5% normal erections on arginine alone, 80% with 80 mg/day Pycnogenol; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/12851125/). Pycnogenol improved endothelium-dependent vasodilation in humans (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/18037769/). The EAU places pine bark extract among antioxidants with only small effects (https://uroweb.org/guidelines/sexual-and-reproductive-health/chapter/management-of-erectile-dysfunction). Solgar's product itself has no trial.

**Safety risks**

- Usually well tolerated. Reported effects are dizziness, drowsiness, rash and stomach complaints (Examine, https://examine.com/supplements/pycnogenol/), plus irritability or lower energy (MSKCC, https://www.mskcc.org/cancer-care/integrative-medicine/herbs/pine-bark-extract).
- Antiplatelet effect: possible bleeding with blood thinners (MSKCC; 100 mg matched 500 mg aspirin against smoking-induced aggregation, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/10498385/), though 27 studies reported no increased bleeding (Examine).
- May lower fasting blood glucose (Examine).
- Following the twice-daily label direction doubles exposure (200 mg/day) with no added benefit for this purpose.
- It has no proven erectile effect on its own. Once it is paired with arginine or citrulline, those products' blood-pressure cautions apply.
- Uncertified, with an unchecked regulatory record, so identity, purity and the absence of hidden drugs rest on the maker's own controls.

**Interactions**

- Anticoagulants and antiplatelets (warfarin, apixaban, rivaroxaban, clopidogrel, aspirin, NSAIDs): additive antiplatelet effect and bleeding risk (MSKCC).
- Diabetes drugs (insulin, sulfonylureas, metformin): may add to glucose lowering (Examine).
- Antihypertensives: Examine lists interactions with blood-pressure-lowering drugs.
- Nitrates and PDE5 inhibitors: no interaction documented for Pycnogenol alone. When paired with arginine or citrulline, avoid it with nitrates and monitor blood pressure with PDE5 inhibitors (Drugs.com, https://www.drugs.com/drug-interactions/arginine-with-sildenafil-232-0-2061-0.html?professional=1).
- Thyroid drugs, SSRIs/MAOIs and sedatives: no interaction documented in the sources reviewed.

**Who should avoid it**

- Men on anticoagulants or antiplatelets, or with a bleeding disorder, unless a doctor agrees; stop before planned surgery.
- Pine allergy.
- Men on insulin or sulfonylureas who are prone to low blood sugar, unless monitored.
- Anyone who would pair it with arginine or citrulline while taking nitrates.
- Men with new, unexplained ED who have not had a cardiovascular check (AUA, https://www.auanet.org/guidelines-and-quality/guidelines/erectile-dysfunction-(ed)-guideline).

**Stop signals**

- Unusual bruising, nosebleeds, or blood in urine or stool.
- Persistent dizziness, headache or stomach upset.
- Rash or other allergic reaction.
- Low-blood-sugar symptoms if taking diabetes medicines.
- No gain of at least 4 IIEF erectile-domain points (2 if ED is mild) after 8-12 weeks on the full Pycnogenol + arginine regimen (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21855209/).

**Challenge reviews**

- *certification-label lens — confirmed; downgrade to: none*
  - Audit basis: raw page captures from this workflow (7-8 Nov 2026), not the verifier's summary; live re-opening was blocked today (network policy, spent WebSearch budget, Firecrawl out of credits until 2026-11-09 05:26 UTC).

  - Directions conflict (Solgar's site: 1 capsule twice daily; retailers: 1-2 daily) and the inactive ingredients differ by listing (one gives microcrystalline cellulose, vegetable cellulose and magnesium stearate; a GNC PDF gives flavour, sweetener, preservatives and colour), which suggests more than one label version. The 100 mg Pycnogenol figure is consistent across all of them.

- *safety-evidence lens — corrected; downgrade to: none*
  - Missed caution: monographs based on Natural Medicines (WebMD) list an immunosuppressant interaction and advise avoiding Pycnogenol in autoimmune disease (theoretical immune stimulation). From my own knowledge; not re-opened this pass.
  - Identity risk: ConsumerLab says pine-bark assays can be fooled by peanut-skin extract and does not test these products. Without a certification, undeclared peanut material cannot be ruled out, which matters for men with a peanut allergy (inference).
  - Solgar's twice-daily direction (200 mg/day) is 2.5 times the trial dose. One capsule a day is enough for the Prelox-type pairing.
  - Solgar's regulatory record was never searched, and the product is uncertified.

### Report rank 11: NOW Foods (NOW Sports) — NOW Sports L-Citrulline Extra Strength 1,200 mg tablets (120)

- **Verifier verdict:** acceptable — A clean single-ingredient citrulline that is easy to buy in the Gulf and reaches the trial dose with 2 tablets, but its 'Informed Sport' claim is not supported by the database and NOW's regulatory record was not checked, so the Informed Sport-listed NOW Sports Arginine & Citrulline or Kaged Citrulline should come first.
- **Product as verified:** NOW Foods (NOW Sports) — NOW Sports L-Citrulline Extra Strength 1,200 mg tablets (120)
- **Product page:** https://www.vitacost.com/products/now-foods-sports-l-citrulline-1-200-mg-120-tablets-59155
- **Where sold:** Gulf: Sporter UAE and Nahdi Saudi Arabia. Also US (Vitacost, Walmart, iHerb), Ireland (Lily's Pharmacy), EU (trufit.eu) and India (1mg). Per discovery and prior research, not re-verified in this run.
- **Label verified:** no. Prior project research recorded 1,200 mg L-citrulline per tablet from the iHerb listing on 7 Nov 2026 (https://www.iherb.com/pr/now-foods-sports-l-citrulline-1-200-mg-120-tablets/59155). Discovery recorded 'L-Citrulline 1.2 g, 120 tablets' from Vitacost. Neither pass, nor this run, read the manufacturer's Supplement Facts panel or directions.
- **Proprietary blend:** no
- **Directions and daily amounts:** 1 tablet = L-citrulline 1,200 mg (1.2 g); 120 tablets. Directions were not captured, so check the label wording on the bottle. 1 tablet a day = 1.2 g; 2 tablets a day = 2.4 g. A single ingredient, with no blend.
- **Dose versus trials (matches):** Cormio 2011 (24 men with mild ED, single-blind, 1 month) used 1.5 g/day: 50% vs 8.3% improved erection hardness from EHS 3 to 4 (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21195829/). One tablet (1.2 g) is 80% of that dose. Two tablets (2.4 g) exceed it, staying within doses shown safe in short studies (up to 6 g/day for 4 weeks; https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/ExerciseAndAthleticPerformance-HealthProfessional/) and near the 3 g doses that raised plasma arginine more than arginine itself did (Schwedhelm 2008; https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2291275/). One tablet also equals the 1.2 g citrulline in Horphag's Prelox R combination (80 mg Pycnogenol + 1.92 g arginine + 1.2 g citrulline + 40 mg oak extract; Stanislavov & Rohdewald 2015, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/25664962; https://www.oatext.com/review-influence-of-combinations-of-pycnogenolr-and-l-arginine-aspartate-on-mens-virility-and-fertility.php).
- **Certification:** None verified. Healthline and an interaction site call it 'Informed Sport certified', but neither prior research (the Informed Sport Nov-2026 PDF list, https://sport.wetestyoutrust.com/sites/default/files/generated-pdf/file.pdf) nor discovery found it in the Informed Sport database, which lists only NOW Sports Arginine & Citrulline powder. ConsumerLab's L-Citrulline Supplements Review (updated 25 Jun 2026) includes it, but its pass/fail is paywalled (discovery), and ConsumerLab does not screen for hidden drugs. NOW's citrulline powder page cites Intertek GMP certification, which is a facility audit rather than product certification (https://www.nowfoods.com/products/sports-nutrition/l-citrulline-pure-powder, prior research). No certifier database could be opened in this run. (certifier page: https://sport.wetestyoutrust.com/supplement-search/brand/now-foods; verified in the certifier's database: no; verifier marked the programme as screening for hidden drugs: no — see the challenge notes, which found PDE5-drug screening confirmed only for BSCG)
- **Manufacturer and regulatory record:** Maker: NOW Foods (USA), described by discovery as a large manufacturer with an in-house laboratory (company claim). Neither prior project research nor discovery recorded any NOW Foods regulatory history. Category context: Examine reports a 2014 recall of a citrulline product that contained no citrulline, and citrulline-malate products below label claim; these brands were not named and the finding is not NOW-specific (https://examine.com/supplements/citrulline/). VERIFICATION LIMIT, this run: the shared WebSearch call limit was reached and the session's network allows only package registries and MCP servers, so FDA warning letters and recalls, Health Canada, TGA and MHRA could not be searched. These limits were not routed around. Allow the hosts, or raise CLAUDE_CODE_MAX_WEB_SEARCHES_PER_SESSION, and re-run. Status: unknown.
- **Evidence summary:** Citrulline-for-ED evidence is a single small, single-blind, non-randomised pilot: Cormio 2011 (24 men with mild ED, 1.5 g/day for 1 month) found 50% vs 8.3% responders, intercourse rising from 1.37 to 2.3 a month, and no adverse events (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21195829/). The Barbonetti 2024 network meta-analysis of 15 RCTs did not find citrulline effective; only L-arginine and PLC+ALC were (https://europepmc.org/article/MED/39279185). Pharmacology: citrulline raises plasma arginine more effectively than oral arginine (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2291275/). Its best use here is as the amino-acid half of a rebuilt Prelox, since Prelox R used 1.2 g/day citrulline (https://europepmc.org/article/MED/25664962). This product itself has no trial.

**Safety risks**

- Stomach discomfort, bloating, cramping or diarrhoea (NIH ODS, https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/ExerciseAndAthleticPerformance-HealthProfessional/; Cleveland Clinic, https://health.clevelandclinic.org/citrulline-benefits). Single doses up to 15 g caused no side effects in healthy men (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/17953788/).
- Long-term safety is unstudied: ODS says studies 'have not adequately assessed the safety of citrulline ... for months at a time', though up to 6 g/day for 4 weeks caused no adverse effects.
- Additive blood-pressure lowering with vasodilators and antihypertensives (Drugs.com; Examine, https://examine.com/supplements/citrulline/).
- Category quality risk: some citrulline products have contained less than claimed, or none (Examine). This tablet's content is not independently certified.
- No drug-screening certification is verified, so the absence of hidden drugs is not independently tested. The prior risk is low for a plain amino-acid tablet from a major brand.

**Interactions**

- Nitrates and NO donors: citrulline may potentiate their hypotensive effect, so do not combine (Drugs.com, https://www.drugs.com/drug-interactions/arginine-with-sildenafil-232-0-2061-0.html?professional=1).
- PDE5 inhibitors: additive vasodilation, so monitor blood pressure (Drugs.com 'moderate'). In one study, 800 mg/day citrulline + resveratrol was taken with PDE5 inhibitors for 1 month without adverse events (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6302103/).
- Antihypertensives and alpha-blockers such as tamsulosin: additive blood-pressure lowering (Drugs.com, https://www.drugs.com/drug-interactions/l-arginine-with-tamsulosin-232-4042-2146-0.html?professional=1; Examine).
- Anticoagulants/antiplatelets and diabetes drugs: no interaction documented for citrulline in the sources reviewed. Because citrulline is converted to arginine, arginine's bleeding and glucose cautions apply in theory (Mayo, https://www.mayoclinic.org/drugs-supplements-l-arginine/art-20364681).
- Thyroid drugs, SSRIs/MAOIs and sedatives: no interaction documented in the sources reviewed.

**Who should avoid it**

- Anyone taking nitrates or other NO donors, including 'poppers'.
- Men with low resting blood pressure, men within 6 months of a heart attack or stroke, and men with unstable heart disease. This is by extension from arginine and the sildenafil-label exclusions (https://www.drugs.com/pro/viagra.html); there is no citrulline-specific trial.
- Men who get dizzy on standing while taking antihypertensives or alpha-blockers, unless a doctor agrees.
- Men with new, unexplained ED who have not had a cardiovascular check (AUA, https://www.auanet.org/guidelines-and-quality/guidelines/erectile-dysfunction-(ed)-guideline).

**Stop signals**

- Dizziness, light-headedness or fainting, especially on standing.
- Chest pain or palpitations.
- An erection lasting more than 4 hours: get emergency care.
- Persistent stomach upset or diarrhoea.
- No gain of at least 4 IIEF erectile-domain points (2 if ED is mild) after 8-12 weeks (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21855209/).

### Report rank 12: NOW Foods (NOW Sports) — NOW Sports Arginine & Citrulline 500 mg/250 mg veg capsules (120 or 240)

- **Verifier verdict:** acceptable — Two disclosed amino acids that reach trial doses at 5-6 capsules a day, from a large maker whose recalls were minor and unrelated, but the Informed Sport listing appears to cover NOW's Arginine & Citrulline powder rather than these capsules and the capsule directions were not captured, so the certified powder is the better choice.
- **Product as verified:** NOW Foods (NOW Sports) — NOW Sports Arginine & Citrulline 500 mg/250 mg veg capsules (120 or 240)
- **Product page:** https://www.mumzworld.com/en/now-foods-arginine-citrulline-capsules-120-s
- **Where sold:** US: HEB. Canada: Vitasave. Saudi Arabia: Nahdi (240 veg capsules). UAE: Mumzworld (120 capsules). UK/EU: not verified.
- **Label verified:** no. Supplement Facts reproduced by Fullscript ('Serving Size: 1 Veg Capsule; L-Arginine 500mg; L-Citrulline 250mg'; https://fullscript.com/en-US/catalog/products/arginine-citrulline). The same 500/250 mg split appears in the product names at HEB, Nahdi and HelloPharmacist, all read via the search index on 8 Nov 2026. NOW's own page and NIH DSLD could not be opened, so directions and other ingredients were not captured.
- **Proprietary blend:** no
- **Directions and daily amounts:** Per veg capsule: L-arginine 500 mg and L-citrulline 250 mg; no other actives listed. Directions were not captured, so check the bottle. 5 capsules/day = 2.5 g arginine + 1.25 g citrulline; 6 capsules/day = 3 g arginine + 1.5 g citrulline, split through the day. Not the same product as NOW Sports Arginine & Citrulline Powder (1.5 g + 1.5 g per level teaspoon), which is the Informed Sport-listed item.
- **Dose versus trials (partial):** Arginine: the positive trials used 2.5 g/day (Gallo 2020, 12 weeks; IIEF-EF +3.1, +5.4 in mild ED), 3 g (El-Wakeel 2020, with sildenafil), 5 g (Chen 1999) and 6 g (Menafra 2022, 98 men, 3 months); 1.5 g/day was no better than placebo (Klotz 1999). Five or six capsules (2.5-3 g) reach the low end of the effective range. Citrulline: 6 capsules give 1.5 g, the Cormio 2011 dose. Against Prelox: 6 capsules give more arginine than the Prelox trials (about 1.6 g) but no Pycnogenol, so they do not reproduce them. Whether 5-6 capsules is within NOW's directions is unconfirmed, and no trial tested this capsule.
- **Certification:** None verified for the capsules. Informed Sport lists 'NOW Sports Arginine & Citrulline', certified 23-Jul-2018 (https://sport.wetestyoutrust.com/supplement-search/now-foods/now-sports-arginine-citrulline). Search-index summaries describe that listing as the powder: batch 3450953 (unflavoured, expires 31-Mar-2028) tested 26-Mar-2026, with earlier tests from Dec 2024 to Nov 2025. Vitacost likewise labels the powder Informed Sport certified (https://www.vitacost.com/now-sports-arginine-citrulline-powder-informed-sport-certified). NOW's Informed Sport entries are form-specific: separate pages exist for Arginine & Ornithine Capsules, L-Arginine Powder and L-Arginine 1000 mg tablets (https://sport.wetestyoutrust.com/supplement-search/brand/now-foods). No capsule entry for Arginine & Citrulline was found, and the capsules are absent from NSF's Certified for Sport directory (8 Nov 2026 scrape). Buy the powder, or confirm the capsule batch number on the listing. (certifier page: none for the capsules (the Informed Sport listing found, https://sport.wetestyoutrust.com/supplement-search/now-foods/now-sports-arginine-citrulline, appears to cover the powder); verified in the certifier's database: no; verifier marked the programme as screening for hidden drugs: no — see the challenge notes, which found PDE5-drug screening confirmed only for BSCG)
- **Manufacturer and regulatory record:** NOW Foods / NOW Health Group is a large US manufacturer. A web search on 8 Nov 2026 found: an older FDA warning letter over possible quintozene (pesticide) in one American ginseng lot, reported as 2005 (https://www.supplysidesj.com/supplement-regulations/now-responds-to-fda-warning-letter); a 2016 voluntary recall of six unrelated supplements (about 165 units) after a label-printing error (https://www.Drugs.com/fda/now-health-group-dietary-supplements-recall-mislabeling-13846.html); an earlier recall of a phosphatidylserine product below label claim (https://wholefoodsmagazine.com/keywords/12602-recall); and 2025 food recalls of nutritional yeast (Class III) and cashews (Class II), per an aggregator (https://recallcheck.net/brand/now-foods). Nothing names an arginine or citrulline product, and no FDA hidden-drug notice for NOW surfaced. Not searched: FDA databases directly, Health Canada, TGA, MHRA. A live re-check was impossible this pass (WebSearch limit used up, WebFetch blocked, Firecrawl unavailable).
- **Evidence summary:** Arginine has the better ED evidence. A 2019 meta-analysis (10 RCTs, 540 men) found it improved mild-to-moderate ED (OR 3.37) (https://europepmc.org/article/MED/30770070). A 2024 network meta-analysis ranked L-arginine the only single nutraceutical with significant efficacy, below sildenafil and tadalafil (https://europepmc.org/article/MED/39279185). The best placebo-controlled trial (Menafra 2022, 6 g/day) gave about a 4-point IIEF-6 gain. The EAU gives arginine a weak recommendation in mild ED. Citrulline rests on one 24-man pilot but raises plasma arginine more efficiently than arginine itself (Schwedhelm 2008). No trial tested this capsule.

**Safety risks**

- GI upset (nausea, abdominal pain, bloating, diarrhoea), headache and gout are listed for arginine (Mayo Clinic). Insomnia occurred in 5% at 2.5 g/day (Gallo 2020). Citrulline occasionally causes bloating or diarrhoea (Cleveland Clinic).
- Recent heart attack: arginine 9 g/day after STEMI was stopped for excess deaths, 8.6% vs 0% (VINTAGE MI); Mayo Clinic advises against it after a recent heart attack.
- Additive blood-pressure lowering with vasodilators and antihypertensives (Drugs.com; Mayo Clinic).
- Arginine can worsen allergies or asthma, and can raise potassium with potassium-sparing diuretics (Mayo Clinic).
- Long-term safety is not established: ED trials of arginine alone ran up to 3 months and of citrulline 1 month. ODS says citrulline has not been assessed for months-long use, though up to 6 g/day for 4 weeks caused no adverse effects.
- No drug-screening certification is verified for this capsule form, though the risk is low for a plain amino-acid capsule from a large maker.

**Interactions**

- Nitrates and NO donors (nitroglycerin, isosorbide, nicorandil, 'poppers'): avoid; additive hypotension (Mayo Clinic; Drugs.com).
- PDE5 inhibitors: additive blood-pressure lowering, rated 'moderate' by Drugs.com; daily arginine 2.5 g + tadalafil 5 mg caused no serious events in 300 men (Gallo 2020).
- Antihypertensives and alpha-blockers (tamsulosin, doxazosin): additive hypotension (Mayo Clinic; Drugs.com; Natural Medicines).
- Potassium-sparing diuretics (spironolactone, amiloride, triamterene): hyperkalaemia (Mayo Clinic).
- Anticoagulants/antiplatelets: possible added bleeding risk with arginine (Mayo Clinic).
- Diabetes drugs: arginine may lower blood sugar (Mayo Clinic).
- Isoproterenol: hypotension (Mayo Clinic).
- Surgery: stop citrulline at least 2 weeks before (Natural Medicines).
- Thyroid drugs, SSRIs/MAOIs and sedatives: none documented for arginine or citrulline (Mayo Clinic; WebMD; Natural Medicines).

**Who should avoid it**

- Anyone taking nitrates or other NO donors, including 'poppers'.
- Men within 6 months of a heart attack or stroke, or with unstable angina, heart failure or low resting blood pressure (VINTAGE MI; sildenafil label).
- Men on potassium-sparing diuretics or with kidney disease or an electrolyte imbalance (Drugs.com). Men with heart, kidney or liver problems should clear citrulline with a clinician (WebMD).
- Men with asthma or severe allergies, or on anticoagulants, unless a doctor agrees (Mayo Clinic).
- Men with new, unexplained ED who have not had a cardiovascular check (AUA).

**Stop signals**

- Dizziness, light-headedness or fainting, especially on standing.
- Chest pain, breathlessness or new palpitations: seek emergency care.
- An erection lasting more than 4 hours: emergency care.
- Persistent bloating, cramping, diarrhoea or heartburn.
- Hives, facial or throat swelling, or wheezing (WebMD).
- Being prescribed nitrates or a new blood-pressure drug, or having surgery scheduled: stop and review.
- A gain of less than 4 IIEF erectile-domain points (2 if ED is mild) after 8-12 weeks (Rosen 2011).

**Challenge reviews**

- *certification-label lens — confirmed; downgrade to: none*
  - Audit basis: raw page captures from this workflow (7-8 Nov 2026), not the verifier's summary; live re-opening was blocked today (network policy, spent WebSearch budget, Firecrawl out of credits until 2026-11-09 05:26 UTC).
  - Correctly marked uncertified. Direct evidence that the Informed Sport listing is the powder: NOW's directly captured tablet listing (8 Nov 2026) links to it with the image label 'NOW Sports Arginine & Citrulline Powder - Bottle' (https://sport.wetestyoutrust.com/supplement-search/now-foods/l-arginine-1000mg-tabs).
  - Not in the NSF directory of 8 Nov 2026 (no NOW product is listed there).
  - Label (500 mg arginine plus 250 mg citrulline per capsule) comes from Fullscript's copy; directions were not captured.

- *safety-evidence lens — confirmed; downgrade to: none*
  - Correctly marked uncertified: the Informed Sport listing covers the powder, not these capsules.
  - Reaching trial doses takes 5-6 capsules a day. The directions were not captured, so this may exceed the label.
  - The Rhim pooling caveat, Cormio's fixed-order design and the ACE inhibitor/ARB potassium caution (inference) all apply.

### Report rank 13: Etixx — Etixx Arginine 1000 (30 tablets)

- **Verifier verdict:** acceptable — It combines a claimed every-batch drug-screening certification with a trial-range 3 g arginine dose, but the claimed listing is unconfirmed here, the added 8.4 mg/day iron is unnecessary for most men and must be avoided with haemochromatosis, the latest visible batch test is from Oct 2025, and it is EU-only, so an iron-free certified arginine or citrulline is the better pick.
- **Product as verified:** Etixx — Etixx Arginine 1000 (30 tablets)
- **Product page:** https://www.etixxsports.com/en/products/arginine-1000
- **Where sold:** EU only: pharmacies and sports retailers in Belgium, the Netherlands, Luxembourg and Spain (per discovery, not re-verified). Not found in the Gulf; not a US product.
- **Label verified:** no. Discovery's reading of etixxsports.com, mapharmacie.lu and promofarma.com. etixxsports.com failed to resolve in this run because of the session network policy, and prior project research did not cover this product.
- **Proprietary blend:** no
- **Directions and daily amounts:** Per discovery: per 3 tablets, L-arginine 3,000 mg and iron 8.4 mg (60% of the EU reference intake, consistent with the 14 mg NRV). Suggested use: 1-3 tablets 1.5 hours before training or competition. At the 3-tablet maximum the daily intake is 3 g L-arginine + 8.4 mg iron. The arginine form (base or HCl) and the excipients were not captured. No blend.
- **Dose versus trials (matches):** 3 g/day L-arginine sits inside the effective range of arginine-only ED trials. It is above the 2.5 g/day in Gallo 2020 (12 weeks; IIEF-EF +3.1 overall, +5.4 in mild ED; https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7261690/). It is below Chen 1999's 5 g/day (https://europepmc.org/article/MED/10233492) and Menafra 2022's 6 g/day (IIEF-6 rose from 20 to 24; https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8995264/). It falls within Rhim 2019's pooled 1.5-5 g/day (OR 3.37; https://europepmc.org/article/MED/30770070), while 1.5 g/day alone was no better than placebo (Klotz 1999; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/10743698/). It also exceeds the roughly 1.6-1.7 g free arginine in the Prelox trial regimen (2.8-3 g arginine aspartate), but it contains no Pycnogenol. The label frames it as a pre-workout product, so 3 tablets daily stays within the stated maximum but not the stated purpose. The iron has no ED trial basis.
- **Certification:** Informed Sport (claimed). Discovery reports a database entry, seen only through the search index of https://sport.wetestyoutrust.com/supplement-search/etixx/arginine-1000: certified 31 Jan 2018; batch L06/25 tested 27 Oct 2025 (expiry 30 Jun 2028) and batch L09/24 tested 12 Dec 2024; no 2026 batch visible. Not confirmed in the database in this run, because the host is network-blocked. Informed Sport tests every batch for more than 285 banned substances (https://sport.wetestyoutrust.com/about/sport-vs-choice); prior research did not confirm whether its panel covers PDE5 inhibitors. (certifier page: https://sport.wetestyoutrust.com/supplement-search/etixx/arginine-1000; verified in the certifier's database: no; verifier marked the programme as screening for hidden drugs: yes — see the challenge notes, which found PDE5-drug screening confirmed only for BSCG)
- **Manufacturer and regulatory record:** Brand: Etixx (Belgian sports-nutrition brand). Neither prior project research nor discovery recorded any regulatory history. VERIFICATION LIMIT, this run: the shared WebSearch call limit was reached and the session's network allows only package registries and MCP servers, so FDA, RASFF, the Belgian food-safety agency (AFSCA/FAVV), Health Canada, TGA and MHRA could not be searched, and the Informed Sport entry could not be opened. These limits were not routed around. Allow the hosts, or raise CLAUDE_CODE_MAX_WEB_SEARCHES_PER_SESSION, and re-run. Status: unknown.
- **Evidence summary:** Arginine is the best-supported single nutraceutical for ED, but its effect is modest. The Rhim 2019 meta-analysis (10 RCTs, 540 men, 1.5-5 g/day) found an OR of 3.37 for improvement, with adverse effects in 8.3% vs 2.3% on placebo (https://europepmc.org/article/MED/30770070). Gallo 2020 (300 men, 12 weeks, 2.5 g/day) found IIEF-EF +3.1 overall, +5.4 in mild ED and +0.1 in severe ED (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7261690/). Menafra 2022 (98 men, 6 g/day, 3 months) found IIEF-6 rising from 20 to 24 (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8995264/). The Barbonetti 2024 network meta-analysis (15 RCTs) found arginine the only effective single nutraceutical (https://europepmc.org/article/MED/39279185). The EAU gives a weak recommendation for daily arginine in mild ED and says the improvement 'could be mild' (https://uroweb.org/guidelines/sexual-and-reproductive-health/chapter/management-of-erectile-dysfunction). There is no ED evidence for iron, and no trial of Etixx Arginine itself.

**Safety risks**

- Vasodilation effects: headache, flushing, dizziness, palpitations and possible hypotension (Drugs.com L-arginine monograph, https://www.drugs.com/npp/l-arginine.html).
- Recent heart attack: an RCT of 9 g/day arginine after STEMI was stopped for excess deaths (8.6% vs 0%; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16391217/). Mayo Clinic says arginine is not recommended after a recent heart attack (https://www.mayoclinic.org/drugs-supplements-l-arginine/art-20364681).
- Iron 8.4 mg/day roughly doubles the 8 mg/day RDA for men (UL 45 mg). Iron supplements can cause upset stomach, constipation and nausea, and lower zinc absorption. People with hereditary haemochromatosis should avoid iron supplements (NIH ODS, https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/Iron-Consumer/).
- High potassium if combined with potassium-sparing diuretics (Mayo Clinic).
- Mild GI upset, insomnia, headache and dyspepsia at trial doses (Gallo 2020: 11 adverse events per 100 men at 2.5 g/day). Doses up to 9 g/day are considered safe for weeks (NIH ODS, https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/ExerciseAndAthleticPerformance-HealthProfessional/).
- Bleeding risk with blood thinners (Mayo Clinic).
- Drug screening is claimed (Informed Sport) but not confirmed in this run, and the latest visible batch test dates from Oct 2025.

**Interactions**

- Nitrates and NO donors (nitroglycerin, isosorbide, nicorandil, 'poppers'): additive hypotension, so do not combine (Mayo; Drugs.com, https://www.drugs.com/drug-interactions/arginine-with-sildenafil-232-0-2061-0.html?professional=1).
- PDE5 inhibitors (sildenafil, tadalafil, vardenafil): additive blood-pressure lowering; Drugs.com rates it 'moderate, monitor'.
- Antihypertensives (ACE inhibitors, ARBs, beta-blockers, calcium-channel blockers) and alpha-blockers such as tamsulosin: additive hypotension (Cleveland Clinic, https://my.clevelandclinic.org/health/drugs/22536-l-arginine; Drugs.com, https://www.drugs.com/drug-interactions/l-arginine-with-tamsulosin-232-4042-2146-0.html?professional=1).
- Potassium-sparing diuretics (spironolactone, amiloride, triamterene): risk of high blood potassium (Mayo).
- Anticoagulants and antiplatelets: increased bleeding risk (Mayo).
- Diabetes drugs (insulin, sulfonylureas, metformin): arginine may lower blood glucose (Mayo).
- Isoproterenol: hypotension (Mayo).
- Thyroid drugs: iron reduces levothyroxine absorption, so separate the doses. Iron also reduces levodopa absorption, and proton pump inhibitors reduce iron absorption (NIH ODS iron fact sheet, https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/Iron-Consumer/).
- SSRIs/MAOIs and sedatives: no interaction documented for arginine or iron in the sources reviewed.

**Who should avoid it**

- Anyone taking nitrates or other NO donors, including 'poppers'.
- Men within 6 months of a heart attack, stroke or life-threatening arrhythmia, or with unstable angina or heart failure (VINTAGE MI; the sildenafil-label exclusions, https://www.drugs.com/pro/viagra.html).
- Men with low resting blood pressure or uncontrolled high blood pressure.
- Men with hereditary haemochromatosis or known iron overload (NIH ODS).
- Men on potassium-sparing diuretics, or with kidney disease or electrolyte imbalance (https://www.drugs.com/drug-interactions/arginine,l-arginine.html).
- Men on levothyroxine, unless the doses are separated.
- Guanidinoacetate methyltransferase deficiency (Cleveland Clinic). Use caution with asthma or allergies (Mayo).
- Men with new, unexplained ED who have not had a cardiovascular check (AUA, https://www.auanet.org/guidelines-and-quality/guidelines/erectile-dysfunction-(ed)-guideline).

**Stop signals**

- Chest pain, palpitations, fainting or marked dizziness: stop and seek medical care.
- An erection lasting more than 4 hours: get emergency care (https://www.drugs.com/pro/viagra.html).
- Constipation, nausea, abdominal pain or black stools that persist (iron), or a raised ferritin on a blood test.
- Persistent GI upset, headache or insomnia.
- Low-blood-sugar symptoms if taking diabetes medicines.
- No gain of at least 4 IIEF erectile-domain points (2 if ED is mild) after 8-12 weeks at 3 g/day (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21855209/).

**Challenge reviews**

- *certification-label lens — corrected; downgrade to: acceptable*
  - Audit basis: raw page captures from this workflow (7-8 Nov 2026), not the verifier's summary; live re-opening was blocked today (network policy, spent WebSearch budget, Firecrawl out of credits until 2026-11-09 05:26 UTC).
  - Certification seen only through search summaries of https://sport.wetestyoutrust.com/supplement-search/etixx/arginine-1000. The indexed copy lists three batches, not two: L06/25 (tested 27-Oct-2025, expires 30-Jun-2028), L09/24 (12-Dec-2024, expires 30-Sep-2027) and L09/23 (14-Nov-2023, expires 30-Sep-2026). The expired L09/23 row shows the snapshot predates October 2026.
  - With the certification unverified, the shortlist's 'screens_for_hidden_drugs: true' should read 'claimed, unconfirmed'.
  - Label (3,000 mg L-arginine plus 8.4 mg iron per 3 tablets; 1-3 tablets 1.5 hours before training) also comes from search summaries; the arginine form and excipients were not captured.

- *safety-evidence lens — corrected; downgrade to: none*
  - The iron arithmetic is overstated. 8.4 mg/day is about one recommended daily allowance for men (8 mg) on its own, not double it. It is added on top of dietary iron, which in men is usually above the allowance already, and that is what matters for haemochromatosis carriers and for timing doses apart from levothyroxine and proton-pump inhibitors.
  - The certification claim was seen only through the search index. The latest batch tested was 27-Oct-2025, with none in 2026, so it is unconfirmed.
  - PDE5 caveat on the Informed Sport screen: PDE5 inhibitors are not WADA-prohibited.
  - The Rhim pooling caveat and the ACE inhibitor/ARB potassium caution (inference) apply. The label presents it as a pre-training product, so taking 3 tablets every day is outside its stated purpose.

### Report rank 14: Bare Performance Nutrition (BPN) — BPN PUMP stimulant-free nitric-oxide powder (Blue Raspberry, Midnight Melon, Pink Lemonade, Prickly Pear; 25 servings)

- **Verifier verdict:** acceptable — It is NSF Certified for Sport (all four flavours verified in NSF's own directory, where no single-ingredient citrulline is listed) and it prints every amount, but a full scoop is a training-pump dose (three times the ED-trial citrulline plus about 0.6 g nitrate and 1 g agmatine, a mix never tested for ED), so treat it as a fallback at about one-third of a scoop and prefer a single-ingredient, Informed Sport-listed citrulline such as Kaged.
- **Product as verified:** Bare Performance Nutrition (BPN) — BPN PUMP stimulant-free nitric-oxide powder (Blue Raspberry, Midnight Melon, Pink Lemonade, Prickly Pear; 25 servings)
- **Product page:** https://uk.bareperformancenutrition.com/products/pump
- **Where sold:** US: HEB and The Feed, plus BPN's US site (not opened). UK: uk.bareperformancenutrition.com, but NSF has no UK listing for PUMP (see certification). Gulf/Middle East: not checked.
- **Label verified:** yes. BPN UK product page headline '4.5G L-CITRULLINE | 2G NO3-T NITRATES | 1G AGMATINE SULFATE' (https://uk.bareperformancenutrition.com/products/pump), plus the full Supplement Facts reproduced by US retailers (https://www.heb.com/product-detail/bare-performance-nutrition-pump-blue-raspberry/16807549; https://thefeed.com/products/bare-performance-naturals-pump-muscle-pump-enhancer), all read via the search index on 8 Nov 2026. BPN's own full panel and directions were not opened directly.
- **Proprietary blend:** no
- **Directions and daily amounts:** Serving 1 scoop; 25 servings. Per scoop: L-citrulline 4.5 g, citrulline peptides (PepForm) 1.5 g, betaine nitrate (NO3-T) 1 g, arginine nitrate (NO3-T) 1 g, agmatine sulfate 1 g, vitamin C 500 mg, protein 1 g, sodium 35 mg. Other ingredients: silica, citric acid, natural flavour, sucralose, potassium chloride, salt, Reb M. Contains milk. No stimulant. My calculations from molecular weights: about 0.34 g nitrate per g of betaine nitrate, and about 0.26 g nitrate + 0.73 g arginine per g of arginine nitrate. One scoop therefore gives about 0.6 g nitrate (about 10 mmol) and 0.73 g arginine. The citrulline content of the peptide form is not stated, and label directions were not captured. For daily ED-type use, about one-third of a scoop gives 1.5 g free citrulline (the trial dose) with about 0.2 g nitrate and 0.33 g agmatine sulfate.
- **Dose versus trials (partial):** Citrulline: 4.5 g per scoop (plus 1.5 g peptides) against 1.5 g/day in the only citrulline ED study (Cormio 2011, 24 men, single-blind; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21195829/), so three times the trial dose before the peptides are counted. ODS notes up to 6 g/day for 4 weeks without adverse effects but lacks longer-term data. Arginine: about 0.73 g, below the 2.5-6 g/day of the positive trials (Gallo 2020; Menafra 2022), and 1.5 g/day was ineffective (Klotz 1999). Nitrate (about 0.6 g) and agmatine (1 g) have no ED trial. The nitrate alone is about twice EFSA's acceptable daily intake of 3.7 mg nitrate/kg body weight for an 80 kg man (about 300 mg/day), before food nitrate is counted (https://www.efsa.europa.eu/en/press/news/170615; not opened in this run; my calculation). Vitamin C at 500 mg is within the 2,000 mg upper limit (ODS). There is no Pycnogenol, so it does not reproduce the Prelox trials, and the combination has never been tested for ED.
- **Certification:** NSF Certified for Sport. All four PUMP flavours appear in NSF's own directory (live scrape of nsfsport.com, 8 Nov 2026): Blue Raspberry (https://www.nsfsport.com/certified-products/listing-detail.php?id=1802800), Midnight Melon (id=1802799), Pink Lemonade (id=1802801) and Prickly Pear (id=1840480). BPN's older Endo Pump is listed separately and was not evaluated here. No single-ingredient citrulline or arginine product appears in the directory. NSF lists UK versions of other BPN products separately, e.g. Electrolytes (UK) id=1703156, Flight (UK) id=1691791 and G.1.M Sport (UK) id=1776746, but there is no PUMP (UK) entry, so UK-site tubs may not be covered. NSF says its mark means testing for prohibited substances and contaminants and that the label matches the contents (https://www.nsfsport.com/our-mark.php). NSF does not publish whether PDE5 inhibitors are on its panel; BSCG's menu names them (https://www.bscg.org/bscg-banned-substance-testing-menu-v070725). Lot pages were not opened, so check the lot in NSF's app. (certifier page: https://www.nsfsport.com/certified-products/listing-detail.php?id=1802800; verified in the certifier's database: yes; verifier marked the programme as screening for hidden drugs: yes — see the challenge notes, which found PDE5-drug screening confirmed only for BSCG)
- **Manufacturer and regulatory record:** Bare Performance Nutrition is a US sports-nutrition brand and a heavy NSF Certified for Sport participant: 95 BPN listings appear in the 8 Nov 2026 directory scrape (https://www.nsfsport.com/certified-products/search-results.php), a documented quality-programme record. No FDA warning-letter, recall, tainted-product, Health Canada, TGA or MHRA search for BPN was run in this workflow, and the project notes record none, so the record is unknown, not clean. A live check was impossible this pass: the WebSearch call limit was used up, WebFetch was blocked for outside hosts, and Firecrawl was unavailable until 9 Nov 2026 05:26 UTC; these limits were not routed around.
- **Evidence summary:** No trial of PUMP or of this combination for ED. Citrulline alone rests on one small single-blind pilot (Cormio 2011). A 2024 network meta-analysis of 15 placebo-controlled RCTs (1,000 men) found arginine, not citrulline, the only single nutraceutical with significant efficacy (https://europepmc.org/article/MED/39279185). Citrulline raises plasma arginine more than oral arginine does (Schwedhelm 2008). The product is designed and marketed as a training 'pump' supplement, stackable with BPN's PRE pre-workout (https://www.stack3d.com/2026/07/bare-performance-nutrition-pump/).

**Safety risks**

- Stacked blood-pressure lowering: citrulline, arginine and inorganic nitrate all feed nitric-oxide production, so light-headedness is more likely, especially with blood-pressure medicines or PDE5 inhibitors (Drugs.com; EFSA).
- Nitrate load of about 0.6 g per scoop (my calculation), roughly twice EFSA's acceptable daily intake for an 80 kg man; one-third of a scoop stays within it (EFSA, not opened in this run).
- Agmatine sulfate 1 g: human safety data are limited to small short studies and case reports, with no long-term record (Examine agmatine page, not opened in this run).
- GI upset (bloating, cramping, diarrhoea) is the commonest citrulline effect and is more likely at multi-gram doses (Cleveland Clinic; ODS).
- Contains milk (allergen) and the sweeteners sucralose and Reb M (HEB label).
- UK-site tubs have no separate NSF listing.
- Arginine cautions apply in principle, though the dose is small (about 0.73 g): avoid after a recent heart attack (VINTAGE MI; Mayo Clinic).

**Interactions**

- Nitrate medicines and NO donors (nitroglycerin, isosorbide, nicorandil, 'poppers'): avoid. Arginine and citrulline may potentiate nitrates and vasodilators (Drugs.com), and this product adds inorganic nitrate on top.
- PDE5 inhibitors: additive blood-pressure lowering (Drugs.com 'moderate' for arginine + sildenafil); this nitrate + citrulline + agmatine mix has never been tested with them.
- Antihypertensives and alpha-blockers (tamsulosin, doxazosin): additive hypotension (Drugs.com; Natural Medicines citrulline monograph).
- Potassium-sparing diuretics: arginine's hyperkalaemia caution applies (Mayo Clinic), and the product also lists potassium chloride (amount not stated).
- Anticoagulants/antiplatelets and diabetes drugs: arginine's bleeding and glucose cautions apply in theory at this small dose (Mayo Clinic); none is listed for citrulline (WebMD).
- Thyroid drugs, SSRIs/MAOIs and sedatives: none documented for citrulline or arginine (WebMD; Mayo Clinic); agmatine has not been studied with any medicine (Examine).
- Surgery: stop citrulline at least 2 weeks before (Natural Medicines).

**Who should avoid it**

- Men taking nitrate medicines or using 'poppers'.
- Men with low blood pressure, or on several blood-pressure drugs or a daily PDE5 inhibitor, unless a doctor supervises.
- Men within 6 months of a heart attack or stroke, or with unstable heart disease (by extension from arginine; VINTAGE MI).
- Men with a milk allergy.
- Men with heart, kidney or liver problems, unless a clinician agrees (WebMD).
- Anyone who wants a single-ingredient, trial-matched product.

**Stop signals**

- Dizziness, light-headedness or fainting, especially on standing.
- Chest pain, breathlessness or palpitations: seek emergency care.
- A headache that persists for days.
- Persistent bloating, cramping or diarrhoea.
- Hives, swelling or wheeze, especially with a milk allergy.
- An erection lasting more than 4 hours: emergency care.
- No benefit after 8-12 weeks (Rosen 2011 minimal clinically important difference).

**Challenge reviews**

- *certification-label lens — confirmed; downgrade to: none*
  - Audit basis: raw page captures from this workflow (7-8 Nov 2026), not the verifier's summary; live re-opening was blocked today (network policy, spent WebSearch budget, Firecrawl out of credits until 2026-11-09 05:26 UTC).
  - Confirmed in the NSF Certified for Sport directory captured 8 Nov 2026: PUMP Blue Raspberry (1802800), Midnight Melon (1802799), Pink Lemonade (1802801) and Prickly Pear (1840480). There is no PUMP (UK) entry, although other BPN products have separate UK listings (Electrolytes 1703156, Flight 1691791, G.1.M. Sport 1776746).
  - The four detail pages, and therefore their lot lists, were not opened, so lot coverage is unknown.
  - Label rests on a third-party Supplement Facts copy and the BPN UK headline (4.5 g L-citrulline, 2 g NO3-T nitrates, 1 g agmatine sulfate); BPN's own panel was not read, and the citrulline content of the 1.5 g PepForm peptides is not stated.
  - Informed Sport and NSF test for substances banned in sport and do not publish PDE5-inhibitor coverage; only BSCG's menu explicitly lists sildenafil/tadalafil analogues (https://www.bscg.org/bscg-banned-substance-testing-menu-v070725).

- *safety-evidence lens — confirmed; downgrade to: none*
  - PDE5 caveat: NSF Certified for Sport screens for substances banned by sports bodies. PDE5 inhibitors are not WADA-prohibited, and NSF does not publish them on its panel.
  - Missed interaction (inference): PUMP contains potassium chloride (amount not stated) as well as arginine. With ACE inhibitors, ARBs or spironolactone, the potassium adds up, so check potassium if kidney function is reduced.
  - Nitrate interplay (inference): the body converts the roughly 0.6 g of inorganic nitrate per scoop into nitrite and nitric oxide. Combining it with nitrate medicines or PDE5 inhibitors could add to their effect and has not been tested. Avoid it with nitrate medicines.
  - The citrulline anchor (Cormio 2011) was fixed-order and single-blind, and the full formula has no ED trial.

### Report rank 15: Jarrow Formulas — Jarrow Formulas Arginine-Citrulline Sustain (120 sustained-release tablets)

- **Verifier verdict:** acceptable — A simple, fully disclosed arginine-plus-citrulline tablet with no red flags in the material reviewed, but even at the label maximum it supplies only 1 g of each amino acid (below every positive trial dose), it is uncertified and Jarrow's regulatory record was not checked, so it is a weak Prelox replacement next to the certified citrulline and arginine powders.
- **Product as verified:** Jarrow Formulas — Jarrow Formulas Arginine-Citrulline Sustain (120 sustained-release tablets)
- **Product page:** https://jarrow.com/products/arginine-citrulline-sustain-120-tablets
- **Where sold:** US: Jarrow, Swanson, Emerson Ecologics, Fullscript. EU: Smeets & Graas (Netherlands). Gulf: Sporter UAE. Australia: Return2Health. Stock was not checked.
- **Label verified:** yes. Jarrow's own product page and Swanson, read via the search index on 8 Nov 2026 (https://jarrow.com/products/arginine-citrulline-sustain-120-tablets; https://www.swansonvitamins.com/p/jarrow-formulas-inc-arginine-citrulline-sustain-120-tabs), with Emerson Ecologics and Smeets & Graas in agreement. One reseller (myGP) prints 1,000 mg of each amino acid with no vitamin C, which conflicts with Jarrow's page and is treated as a listing error. The other ingredients were not captured.
- **Proprietary blend:** no
- **Directions and daily amounts:** Serving 2 tablets; 60 servings. Per 2 tablets: vitamin C (as calcium ascorbate) 10 mg, L-arginine 500 mg, L-citrulline 500 mg. Sustained release, which one listing says lasts about 8 hours. Directions: 2 tablets once or twice daily. The label maximum (4 tablets) gives 1 g arginine + 1 g citrulline + 20 mg vitamin C. Reaching the citrulline trial dose needs 6 tablets (1.5 g + 1.5 g), 50% above the label maximum.
- **Dose versus trials (underdosed):** Citrulline: 1 g/day at the label maximum versus 1.5 g/day in Cormio 2011, i.e. two-thirds of the trial dose (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21195829/). Arginine: 1 g/day versus 2.5-6 g/day in the positive trials (Gallo 2020; Menafra 2022); even 1.5 g/day was no better than placebo (Klotz 1999, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/10743698/). Against Prelox, it gives less arginine than the 4-tablet trial regimen (about 1.6 g), adds citrulline, and has no Pycnogenol. The sustained-release form has no ED trial. It is underdosed within its own directions.
- **Certification:** None found. Jarrow has no product in NSF's Certified for Sport directory (live scrape of 2,625 products, 8 Nov 2026). No Informed Sport listing turned up in discovery searches of that database, and no certification claim was seen for this product. USP, BSCG and NSF/ANSI 173 were not searched exhaustively this run. (certifier page: none; verified in the certifier's database: no; verifier marked the programme as screening for hidden drugs: no — see the challenge notes, which found PDE5-drug screening confirmed only for BSCG)
- **Manufacturer and regulatory record:** Jarrow Formulas is an established US supplement brand. No search for FDA warning letters, recalls, tainted-product notices, or Health Canada, TGA or MHRA alerts naming Jarrow was run in this workflow, and the project notes record none; an earlier discovery pass listed Jarrow as still to check. Status: unknown, not clean. A live check was impossible this pass: the WebSearch limit was used up, WebFetch was blocked for outside hosts, and Firecrawl was unavailable until 9 Nov 2026 05:26 UTC; these limits were not routed around.
- **Evidence summary:** Arginine helps mild-to-moderate ED in meta-analyses (Rhim 2019, OR 3.37, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/30770070; Barbonetti 2024, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/39279185), but only at 2.5-6 g/day; 1.5 g/day failed. Citrulline rests on one 24-man pilot at 1.5 g/day, though it raises plasma arginine efficiently (Schwedhelm 2008). No trial tested this product, its sustained-release form or this dose.

**Safety risks**

- At the label dose (1 g of each amino acid), vasodilatory and GI effects are likely small; risks rise if more tablets are taken to reach trial doses.
- GI upset, headache and gout are listed for arginine (Mayo Clinic); citrulline occasionally causes bloating or diarrhoea (Cleveland Clinic).
- Recent heart attack: arginine 9 g/day after STEMI was stopped for excess deaths (VINTAGE MI); Mayo Clinic advises against arginine after a recent heart attack.
- Additive blood-pressure lowering with vasodilators and antihypertensives; hyperkalaemia with potassium-sparing diuretics; may worsen asthma (Mayo Clinic; Drugs.com).
- No drug-screening certification; long-term safety beyond 1-3 months of trial use is not established (ODS).

**Interactions**

- Nitrates and NO donors (including 'poppers'): avoid; additive hypotension (Mayo Clinic; Drugs.com).
- PDE5 inhibitors: additive blood-pressure lowering, Drugs.com 'moderate' (Gallo 2020 found no serious events with tadalafil).
- Antihypertensives and alpha-blockers (tamsulosin, doxazosin): additive hypotension (Drugs.com; Natural Medicines).
- Potassium-sparing diuretics: hyperkalaemia (Mayo Clinic).
- Anticoagulants/antiplatelets: possible added bleeding risk; diabetes drugs: may lower glucose; isoproterenol: hypotension (Mayo Clinic).
- Surgery: stop citrulline at least 2 weeks before (Natural Medicines).
- Thyroid drugs, SSRIs/MAOIs and sedatives: none documented (Mayo Clinic; WebMD).

**Who should avoid it**

- Anyone taking nitrates or other NO donors, including 'poppers'.
- Men within 6 months of a heart attack or stroke, or with unstable heart disease or low blood pressure (VINTAGE MI; sildenafil label).
- Men on potassium-sparing diuretics or with kidney disease (Drugs.com; WebMD).
- Men with asthma or severe allergies, or on anticoagulants, unless a doctor agrees (Mayo Clinic).
- Men with new, unexplained ED who have not had a cardiovascular check (AUA).

**Stop signals**

- Dizziness or fainting, especially on standing.
- Chest pain, breathlessness or new palpitations: seek emergency care.
- An erection lasting more than 4 hours: emergency care.
- Persistent GI upset.
- Hives, swelling or wheezing.
- No benefit after 8-12 weeks (Rosen 2011).

**Challenge reviews**

- *certification-label lens — confirmed; downgrade to: none*
  - Audit basis: raw page captures from this workflow (7-8 Nov 2026), not the verifier's summary; live re-opening was blocked today (network policy, spent WebSearch budget, Firecrawl out of credits until 2026-11-09 05:26 UTC).
  - No certification: Jarrow has no product in the NSF directory captured 8 Nov 2026, and no Informed Sport listing turned up.
  - Label (per 2 tablets: L-arginine 500 mg, L-citrulline 500 mg, vitamin C 10 mg; 2 tablets once or twice daily) from Jarrow's page via search index; the myGP panel (1,000 mg of each, no vitamin C) conflicts and may reflect an older or different version.

- *safety-evidence lens — confirmed; downgrade to: none*
  - Correctly flagged as underdosed. Even the label maximum of 4 tablets gives 1 g arginine plus 1 g citrulline, below every positive trial, and taking 6 tablets to reach 1.5 g of citrulline exceeds the label.
  - It is uncertified, and Jarrow's FDA and recall record was never searched, so 'no red flags' means only that no search was done.
  - The ACE inhibitor/ARB potassium caution (inference) and the Rhim and Cormio evidence caveats apply.
