### Avoid list: Herbalife Nutrition (Horphag Research licensee) — Herbalife Prelox Blue (60 tablets)

- **Verifier verdict:** avoid — It delivers the Prelox actives at close to the trial dose, but it has no certification, every NIH-registered label is marked off-market so the current formula is unconfirmed, it adds titanium dioxide and synthetic dyes, it is sold only through MLM distributors, and LiverTox lists Herbalife products as an established though unexplained cause of liver injury, so Pharma Nord Prelox is the better source of this formula.
- **Product as verified:** Herbalife Nutrition (Horphag Research licensee) — Herbalife Prelox Blue (60 tablets)
- **Product page:** https://www.herbalife.com/en-us/u/products/prelox-blue-60-tablets-0039
- **Where sold:** US and Singapore: listed on Herbalife's US pages (English and Spanish catalogues) and its Singapore page. The label says it is 'only available through Herbalife Nutrition Independent Distributors' (https://api.ods.od.nih.gov/dsld/v9/label/270985). UK/EU: not found; Horphag's European Prelox licensee is Pharma Nord (https://www.prelox.com/shop). Gulf/Middle East: not checked.
- **Label verified:** no. NIH DSLD label 270985 (Herbalife Nutrition 'Prelox Blue', copyright 2021; entered 21 Jul 2022; marked off-market 24 Sep 2024), read in full in this workflow on 8 Nov 2026 (https://api.ods.od.nih.gov/dsld/v9/label/270985). DSLD holds four Prelox Blue labels and all are off-market (https://api.ods.od.nih.gov/dsld/v9/search-filter?q=prelox&size=30): 2643 (2011-2014) and 38598 (2014-2021) hid the actives in a 'Prelox Proprietary Blend', while 250720 (2021-2022) and 270985 itemise them. Herbalife's current Singapore page lists the same four actives, and it and the US Spanish catalogue give the same direction (read via the search index). Their milligrams were not captured, so the bottle on sale now could not be matched to the 2022 panel.
- **Proprietary blend:** no
- **Directions and daily amounts:** Serving 2 tablets. 'Recommended use: Take 2 tablets 2 times a day' (4 tablets/day, with no step-down). Per 2 tablets: L-arginine HCl 760 mg, aspartic acid 640 mg, L-taurine 90 mg, Pycnogenol 40 mg. Per day (4 tablets): L-arginine HCl 1,520 mg (about 1.26 g free L-arginine; my calculation, 174.2/210.7), aspartic acid 1,280 mg, taurine 180 mg, Pycnogenol 80 mg. Other ingredients: corn starch, microcrystalline cellulose, croscarmellose sodium, hydroxypropyl methylcellulose, magnesium stearate, titanium dioxide, silicon dioxide, triacetin, acacia gum, FD&C Blue #1 and FD&C Blue #2. No stimulant or hormone. The label carries a paid endorsement: Nobel laureate Louis Ignarro 'receives compensation for his endorsement'.
- **Dose versus trials (partial):** Trial regimen: Ledda 2010 (124 men, 6 months) and Stanislavov 2008 (50 men, crossover) used 4 tablets/day of 20 mg Pycnogenol + 700 mg L-arginine aspartate. That is 80 mg Pycnogenol + 2.8-3 g arginine aspartate, about 1.6-1.7 g arginine + 1.2-1.3 g aspartate (Stanislavov 2003: 3 g = 1.7 g arginine) (Tian 2023 Table 1, https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10614297/; https://europepmc.org/article/MED/20184576; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/17703218/; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/12851125/). Prelox Blue at its directed 4 tablets: Pycnogenol 80 mg (match); free arginine about 1.26 g (about 75-80% of the trial amount, given as the HCl salt plus free aspartic acid rather than the aspartate salt); aspartic acid 1.28 g (match); taurine 180 mg (not described in the trials; Pharma Nord's tablet also contains it). The nearest trial to this salt form is Aoki 2012 (60 mg Pycnogenol + 690 mg arginine + 552 mg aspartic acid, 8 weeks): the IIEF-5 total improved, but the erectile domain was not significantly better than placebo (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21618639/). Arginine alone at 1.5 g/day was no better than placebo (Klotz 1999, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/10743698/), so any effect rests on the Pycnogenol combination. No trial tested Herbalife's tablet.
- **Certification:** None. Prelox Blue is absent from NSF's Certified for Sport directory (live scrape of all 2,625 listed products from nsfsport.com, 8 Nov 2026). Herbalife's NSF-listed items are all Herbalife24 sports products such as CR7 Drive, Rebuild Strength and Creatine (e.g. https://www.nsfsport.com/certified-products/listing-detail.php?id=1786044). BSCG's certified-lot page also showed only Herbalife 24 products on its first page (https://www.bscg.org/certified-drug-free-database). No Informed Sport, USP or NSF/ANSI 173 listing was found, though those databases could not be searched exhaustively in this run. (certifier page: none; verified in the certifier's database: no; verifier marked the programme as screening for hidden drugs: no — see the challenge notes, which found PDE5-drug screening confirmed only for BSCG)
- **Manufacturer and regulatory record:** Herbalife Nutrition is a US multi-level-marketing company. This product is sold only through its independent distributors, under licence of US patent 6,117,872; Prelox and Pycnogenol are Horphag trademarks (DSLD 270985). Liver: NIH LiverTox reports many cases of acute, clinically apparent liver injury in people taking Herbalife products. It notes the link has often been controversial, the responsible ingredients are unidentified, and cases appear to have fallen; a copy of the entry gives likelihood score A, a well-established cause (https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK548447/). A Swiss hospital series of severe cases included one fulminant liver failure that needed a transplant (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/17692989/). A 2015 critical review of 53 published cases concluded causality could not be proven (https://revistas.usp.br/bjps/article/view/118628), and CIOMS-based re-analyses judged causality probable in only one re-exposure case (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23898368/). None of these reports names Prelox Blue, so the signal is brand-level. Hidden drugs: no Prelox entry was found on FDA's 441-entry tainted sexual-enhancement list (prior project keyword checks; https://www.fda.gov/drugs/medication-health-fraud-notifications/sexual-enhancement-and-energy-product-notifications). Not searched: FDA warning letters and recalls for Herbalife, Health Canada, TGA, MHRA. Limits this pass: the shared WebSearch call limit was used up; WebFetch could not resolve api.ods.od.nih.gov or www.herbalife.com, which must be allowed in the session's network settings; and Firecrawl was unavailable until 9 Nov 2026 05:26 UTC. These were not routed around.
- **Evidence summary:** There are three small double-blind RCTs of Pycnogenol + L-arginine aspartate, all with Horphag-linked authors. Ledda 2010: IIEF erectile domain 15.2 to 25.2 at 3 months and 27.1 at 6 months, versus 19.1 and 19.0 on placebo. Stanislavov 2008 (crossover): erectile function restored to normal. Aoki 2012 used a lower dose of free arginine + aspartic acid. The Tian 2023 meta-analysis found IIEF-EF +8.9 versus placebo (2 trials) with no adverse events, but risk of bias unclear to high (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10614297/). There are no data beyond 6 months, in severe ED, or for Herbalife's own tablet. The EAU gives only a weak recommendation to arginine in mild ED and does not endorse Prelox (https://uroweb.org/guidelines/sexual-and-reproductive-health/chapter/management-of-erectile-dysfunction).

**Safety risks**

- Vasodilation effects: headache, flushing, dizziness, palpitations (Drugs.com L-arginine monograph); Pharma Nord's German leaflet for the same actives warns of initial facial flushing or headache.
- Recent heart attack: arginine 9 g/day after STEMI was stopped for excess deaths, 8.6% vs 0% (VINTAGE MI); Mayo Clinic advises against arginine after a recent heart attack. This product supplies much less (about 1.26 g/day), but the caution stands.
- Bleeding: Pycnogenol inhibits platelet aggregation (100 mg matched 500 mg aspirin in smokers, Putter 1999; MSKCC lists an anticoagulant/antiplatelet interaction), although 27+ studies reported no increased bleeding (Examine).
- Brand-level liver-injury signal (LiverTox, culprit ingredients unidentified); no reported case names Prelox Blue.
- Colours: titanium dioxide plus FD&C Blue #1 and #2 (DSLD 270985). EFSA concluded in 2021 that titanium dioxide (E171) can no longer be considered safe as a food additive (not opened in this run). The exposure is small, but these colours serve no purpose.
- Current formula unconfirmed: every DSLD Prelox Blue label is marked off-market.
- No drug-screening certification, so the absence of hidden PDE5 inhibitors is not independently tested, although no adulteration report exists for Prelox.
- High potassium with potassium-sparing diuretics (Mayo Clinic). Mild GI upset or insomnia is possible (Gallo 2020: 11 adverse events per 100 men at 2.5 g/day arginine). Taurine at 180 mg/day is far below EFSA's no-adverse-effect level of 1,000 mg/kg/day. No safety data exist beyond 6 months.

**Interactions**

- Nitrates and NO donors (nitroglycerin, isosorbide, nicorandil, amyl nitrite 'poppers'): do not combine. Additive hypotension (Mayo Clinic); Drugs.com says arginine or citrulline 'may potentiate the hypotensive effects of nitrates and vasodilators'.
- PDE5 inhibitors (sildenafil, tadalafil, vardenafil): additive blood-pressure lowering; Drugs.com rates arginine + sildenafil 'moderate'. Arginine 2.5 g + tadalafil 5 mg daily in 300 men caused more mild side effects but no serious events (Gallo 2020).
- Antihypertensives (ACE inhibitors, ARBs, beta-blockers, calcium-channel blockers) and alpha-blockers such as tamsulosin or doxazosin: additive hypotension (Cleveland Clinic; Drugs.com rates tamsulosin 'moderate').
- Potassium-sparing diuretics (spironolactone, amiloride, triamterene): hyperkalaemia (Mayo Clinic).
- Anticoagulants and antiplatelets (warfarin, apixaban, rivaroxaban, clopidogrel, aspirin, NSAIDs): added bleeding risk (Mayo Clinic for arginine; MSKCC for pine bark).
- Diabetes drugs: arginine and Pycnogenol may lower blood glucose, so doses may need adjusting (Mayo Clinic; Examine).
- Isoproterenol: hypotension (Mayo Clinic).
- Thyroid drugs, SSRIs/MAOIs and sedatives: no interaction documented for arginine, Pycnogenol or taurine in the sources reviewed (Mayo Clinic, Drugs.com, MSKCC, Examine).

**Who should avoid it**

- Anyone taking nitrates or other NO donors, including 'poppers'.
- Men within 6 months of a heart attack, stroke or life-threatening arrhythmia, or with unstable angina, heart failure, resting hypotension or uncontrolled hypertension (VINTAGE MI; cardiovascular exclusions in the sildenafil label).
- Men with liver disease or a past supplement-related liver injury, given the brand-level LiverTox signal.
- Men on anticoagulants or antiplatelets, or with a bleeding disorder, unless a doctor agrees. Stop before planned surgery.
- Men on potassium-sparing diuretics, or with kidney disease or an electrolyte imbalance (Drugs.com disease interactions).
- Men with a pine allergy or guanidinoacetate methyltransferase deficiency (Cleveland Clinic); caution with asthma or allergies (Mayo Clinic).
- Men with new, unexplained ED who have not had a cardiovascular check (AUA).

**Stop signals**

- Chest pain, palpitations, fainting or marked dizziness: stop and seek care.
- An erection lasting more than 4 hours: emergency care (sildenafil label warning).
- Yellow skin or eyes, dark urine, pale stools, itching, right-upper abdominal pain or unusual fatigue: stop and get liver tests (LiverTox).
- Unusual bruising, nosebleeds, or blood in urine or stool.
- Headache, flushing or stomach upset persisting beyond 2 weeks, or a rash or swelling.
- A gain of less than 4 IIEF erectile-domain points (2 if ED is mild) after 8-12 weeks: stop (Rosen 2011 minimal clinically important difference).

### Avoid list: PODIUM Nutrition — PODIUM L-Citrulline (unflavoured powder)

- **Verifier verdict:** avoid — The ingredient raises no safety red flag. But the product's Informed Sport certification was voluntarily withdrawn on 29 Sep 2026, so new batches are untested, and its label could not be verified. A currently certified citrulline, such as NOW Sports Arginine & Citrulline, is the better pick.
- **Product as verified:** PODIUM Nutrition — PODIUM L-Citrulline (unflavoured powder)
- **Product page:** https://thefeed.com/products/podium-l-citrulline
- **Where sold:** Informed Sport's listing gives regional availability as Europe, North America and Oceania, with purchase links to the brand store (321podium.com) and GNC. The Feed (US) also lists it. No specific UK, EU or Gulf retailer was verified.
- **Label verified:** no. Not retrieved. The brand store, The Feed, GNC and NIH DSLD could not be opened this session: the web-search budget was used up, the network policy blocked these hosts, and Firecrawl credits had run out. The Informed Sport page shows only front-label images, with no amounts.
- **Proprietary blend:** no
- **Directions and daily amounts:** Not verified. The grams of L-citrulline per serving, servings per container, other ingredients and directions could not be read. Before use, check on the tub that:
- one serving gives at least 1.5 g L-citrulline;
- plain citrulline, not citrulline malate, is the only ingredient. Citrulline malate is only about 57% citrulline by weight [https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/ExerciseAndAthleticPerformance-HealthProfessional/].
- **Dose versus trials (unknown):** Cannot be compared, because the per-serving amount was not verified.
The only citrulline-alone ED study used 1.5 g/day for 1 month (Cormio 2011, single-blind, 24 men with mild ED: hardness improved in 50% vs 8.3% on placebo) [https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21195829/]. A lower dose of 800 mg/day was tested only together with 300 mg resveratrol (Shirai 2018) [https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6302103/].
A plain citrulline powder usually makes a 1.5-3 g serving easy, but that has to be read from this label.
- **Certification:** Informed Sport: WITHDRAWN. The certifier's own listing, read directly on 8 Nov 2026, states: 'This product was voluntarily withdrawn from the Informed Sport programme on the date listed below. Any batches produced since this date will not be tested. Previously tested batches will remain on the website until they expire.'
- Date withdrawn: 29-Sep-2026.
- Date certified: 01-May-2023.
- Only one batch is still listed: 202500228 (unflavoured, expires 31-Mar-2027, tested 29-Apr-2025).
Search-engine summaries and Informed Sport's November 2026 product list still describe it as certified; that is out of date.
PODIUM L-Citrulline is also not among PODIUM's 23 NSF Certified for Sport listings (creatine, electrolytes, pre-workouts, whey) in the 2,625-listing NSF directory captured on 8 Nov 2026 [https://www.nsfsport.com/certified-products/]. So no current lot is verifiably drug-screened, apart from batch 202500228. (certifier page: https://sport.wetestyoutrust.com/supplement-search/podium/podium-l-citrulline; verified in the certifier's database: no; verifier marked the programme as screening for hidden drugs: no — see the challenge notes, which found PDE5-drug screening confirmed only for BSCG)
- **Manufacturer and regulatory record:** Company: PODIUM Nutrition (brand store 321podium.com).
Quality signal: 23 other PODIUM products are NSF Certified for Sport, for example PODIUM Creatine Monohydrate, listing 1786158 [https://www.nsfsport.com/certified-products/listing-detail.php?id=1786158]. So the brand uses drug-screening certification for part of its range.
Regulatory record: none found, but it was not actually searched. FDA's warning-letter, enforcement-report and tainted-product databases, Health Canada, TGA and MHRA were all unreachable this session: the web-search budget was used up, the network policy blocked regulator hosts, and Firecrawl credits had run out until 9 Nov 2026 05:26 UTC. No adverse item came up in the workflow's earlier certifier searches.
- **Evidence summary:** The body converts citrulline to arginine, and oral citrulline raises plasma arginine more effectively than oral arginine (Schwedhelm 2008) [https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2291275/]. But its erectile evidence is a single 24-man single-blind pilot at 1.5 g/day, whose authors describe it as less effective than PDE5 inhibitors (Cormio 2011) [https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21195829/]. No guideline endorses citrulline; the EAU's weak recommendation covers only arginine or ginseng [https://uroweb.org/guidelines/sexual-and-reproductive-health/chapter/management-of-erectile-dysfunction].

**Safety risks**

- Gastrointestinal discomfort: 6 of 41 weight lifters reported stomach discomfort after 8 g citrulline malate. NIH ODS reports few safety concerns up to 9 g for 1 day or 6 g/day for 16 days, but safety over months of use has not been adequately assessed [https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/ExerciseAndAthleticPerformance-HealthProfessional/].
- Lowers blood pressure; arginine or citrulline may potentiate nitrates and vasodilators [https://www.drugs.com/drug-interactions/arginine-with-sildenafil-232-0-2061-0.html?professional=1; https://health.clevelandclinic.org/citrulline-benefits].
- Certification has lapsed: Informed Sport does not test batches made after 29 Sep 2026 [https://sport.wetestyoutrust.com/supplement-search/podium/podium-l-citrulline].
- The label was not verified, so the dose, the form (citrulline or citrulline malate) and any flavourings or excipients are unknown.

**Interactions**

- Nitrates and other NO donors (nitroglycerin, isosorbide, amyl nitrite 'poppers'): avoid, because of additive hypotension [https://www.drugs.com/drug-interactions/arginine-with-sildenafil-232-0-2061-0.html?professional=1].
- PDE5 inhibitors (sildenafil, tadalafil): additive blood-pressure lowering; combine only if a doctor agrees [https://www.drugs.com/drug-interactions/arginine-with-sildenafil-232-0-2061-0.html?professional=1].
- Antihypertensives and alpha-blockers: additive hypotension, by the same mechanism as arginine [https://health.clevelandclinic.org/citrulline-benefits; https://my.clevelandclinic.org/health/drugs/22536-l-arginine].
- Anticoagulants and antiplatelets, diabetes drugs, thyroid drugs, SSRIs, MAOIs and sedatives: no interaction documented for citrulline in the sources reviewed (NIH ODS, Cleveland Clinic).

**Who should avoid it**

- Men taking nitrates or other NO-donor drugs.
- Men with low blood pressure, or on several blood-pressure drugs, an alpha-blocker or a PDE5 inhibitor, unless a clinician supervises.
- Men with a recent heart attack or unstable heart disease, unless a cardiologist agrees. Citrulline raises plasma arginine, and arginine is not recommended after an acute MI [https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2291275/; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16391217/].
- Men with new, unexplained ED who have not had a cardiovascular check [https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16854949/].

**Stop signals**

- Dizziness, light-headedness or fainting.
- Chest pain, breathlessness or palpitations: get emergency care.
- Persistent stomach upset, bloating or diarrhoea.
- Rash, swelling or wheezing, which suggest an allergic reaction.
- No improvement after 1-2 months at 1.5 g/day or more; the pilot study measured its effect at 1 month [https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21195829/].

### Avoid list: Crown Sport Nutrition — Nitrates 600 + Citrulline (gel)

- **Verifier verdict:** avoid — Dietary nitrate has no erectile-function trial. The label (nitrate and citrulline amounts and other contents) and the current Informed Sport status could not be verified. And it is a sports gel rather than a daily product. A certified plain citrulline is the better Prelox substitute.
- **Product as verified:** Crown Sport Nutrition — Nitrates 600 + Citrulline (gel)
- **Product page:** https://sport.wetestyoutrust.com/supplement-search/crown-sport-nutrition/nitrates-600-citrulline
- **Where sold:** Not verified. The brand's origin and retailers, and the listing's regional availability, could not be read this session. No US, UK or Gulf retailer was confirmed.
- **Label verified:** no. Not retrieved. Only search-engine summaries of the Informed Sport listing were available (8 Nov 2026; the live page was not opened). They describe a gel combining beetroot-derived nitrate with L-citrulline DL-malate, and one summary says it supplies 6,000 mg L-citrulline per serving. The '600' in the name probably refers to mg of nitrate. None of this is confirmed.
- **Proprietary blend:** no
- **Directions and daily amounts:** Not verified. The nitrate amount and source, the citrulline (as DL-malate) amount, any carbohydrate, sweetener or caffeine, the serving size and the directions could not be read. Citrulline malate is only about 57% citrulline by weight (NIH ODS) [https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/ExerciseAndAthleticPerformance-HealthProfessional/], so a citrulline-malate figure overstates the citrulline dose.
- **Dose versus trials (unknown):** Nitrate:
- The project's literature review found no randomized or controlled trial of dietary nitrate or beetroot with an erectile-function outcome. A consumer-health review states there is no evidence that beetroot works for ED [https://ro.co/erectile-dysfunction/beetroot-beet-juice-for-men/].
- Vascular studies typically used about 500 mL beetroot juice (310-682 mg nitrate), once before exercise or daily for up to 15 days (NIH ODS) [https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/ExerciseAndAthleticPerformance-HealthProfessional/].
- Nitrate improved flow-mediated dilation in a dose-related way (Lara 2016 meta-analysis; Velmurugan 2016, +1.1 points over 6 weeks) [https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25764393/; https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4691670/], but not in treated hypertensive adults (Fejes 2024) [https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11034575/].
Citrulline:
- The ED reference dose is 1.5 g/day (Cormio 2011) [https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21195829/].
- If the summary's 6,000 mg refers to citrulline DL-malate, that would be about 3.4 g citrulline, above the trial dose, but this is unconfirmed.
The only chronic citrulline + nitrate trial in men (3 g citrulline and/or 100 mg nitrate a day for 9 weeks) measured testosterone and cortisol, not erections [https://www.mdpi.com/2072-6643/17/24/3954].
- **Certification:** Informed Sport, indexed but unconfirmed. The certifier's database has a listing for this product, and search-engine summaries of it (8 Nov 2026) say all batches are Informed Sport certified. But the live page could not be opened, and no batch numbers, test dates or withdrawal notice were captured. Indexed copies can be stale: PODIUM L-Citrulline's indexed copy said 'certified' while its live page shows it was withdrawn on 29 Sep 2026. So the current status is unconfirmed.
If active, Informed Sport tests every batch for more than 285 substances banned in sport [https://sport.wetestyoutrust.com/about/sport-vs-choice]; whether that covers PDE5 inhibitors is unconfirmed.
Not in the NSF Certified for Sport directory captured on 8 Nov 2026 [https://www.nsfsport.com/certified-products/]. (certifier page: https://sport.wetestyoutrust.com/supplement-search/crown-sport-nutrition/nitrates-600-citrulline; verified in the certifier's database: no; verifier marked the programme as screening for hidden drugs: yes — see the challenge notes, which found PDE5-drug screening confirmed only for BSCG)
- **Manufacturer and regulatory record:** Company: Crown Sport Nutrition; company details were not captured this sweep.
Regulatory record: none found, but it was not searched. The FDA, Health Canada, TGA, MHRA and EU RASFF databases were unreachable this session: the web-search budget was used up, the network policy blocked them, and Firecrawl credits had run out until 9 Nov 2026 05:26 UTC. No adverse item came up in the workflow's certifier searches.
- **Evidence summary:** There is no erectile-function trial for dietary nitrate. The case rests on vascular measures: better flow-mediated dilation, and lower blood pressure in some trials but not all [https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25764393/; https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11034575/]. Citrulline's ED evidence is one 24-man single-blind pilot at 1.5 g/day [https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21195829/]. No guideline endorses either for ED; the EAU's weak recommendation covers only arginine or ginseng [https://uroweb.org/guidelines/sexual-and-reproductive-health/chapter/management-of-erectile-dysfunction].

**Safety risks**

- Long-term use is unstudied. NIH ODS found no safety concerns for about 2 cups/day of beetroot juice (roughly 310-682 mg nitrate) for several weeks, but notes that longer-term supplementation has not been researched [https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/ExerciseAndAthleticPerformance-HealthProfessional/].
- Nitrate and citrulline both act on the nitric-oxide pathway, so their blood-pressure lowering may add up. Dizziness is possible, especially with blood-pressure drugs [https://www.drugs.com/drug-interactions/arginine-with-sildenafil-232-0-2061-0.html?professional=1].
- Citrulline can cause stomach discomfort (6 of 41 weight lifters) [https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/ExerciseAndAthleticPerformance-HealthProfessional/].
- Beeturia (pink or red urine) is harmless but can be mistaken for blood [https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/ExerciseAndAthleticPerformance-HealthProfessional/].
- Unknown composition: as a sports gel it may contain carbohydrate, sweeteners or caffeine, and none of these could be checked.
- The Informed Sport status of current batches is unconfirmed.

**Interactions**

- Nitrate drugs (nitroglycerin, isosorbide mono- or dinitrate, nicorandil) and amyl nitrite 'poppers': avoid. Vasodilation and hypotension add up, because citrulline potentiates nitrates and vasodilators [https://www.drugs.com/drug-interactions/arginine-with-sildenafil-232-0-2061-0.html?professional=1].
- PDE5 inhibitors (sildenafil, tadalafil): no study has tested dietary nitrate with a PDE5 inhibitor, and citrulline adds to their blood-pressure lowering; combine only if a doctor agrees [https://www.drugs.com/drug-interactions/arginine-with-sildenafil-232-0-2061-0.html?professional=1].
- Antihypertensives and alpha-blockers: additive blood-pressure lowering is plausible, because dietary nitrate lowered blood pressure in some trials [https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25764393/; https://health.clevelandclinic.org/citrulline-benefits].
- Anticoagulants and antiplatelets: nitrate-rich juice reduced platelet-monocyte aggregates in one trial, so a small additive antiplatelet effect is theoretical [https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4691670/].
- Diabetes drugs: no interaction documented; check the gel's carbohydrate content.
- Thyroid drugs, SSRIs, MAOIs and sedatives: no interaction documented for nitrate or citrulline in the sources reviewed.

**Who should avoid it**

- Men taking nitrate medicines or using poppers.
- Men with low blood pressure, or on several blood-pressure drugs, an alpha-blocker or a PDE5 inhibitor, unless a clinician agrees.
- Anyone looking for a daily vascular supplement to take for months, because daily nitrate supplementation beyond several weeks is unstudied [https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/ExerciseAndAthleticPerformance-HealthProfessional/].
- Men with new, unexplained ED who have not had a cardiovascular check [https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16854949/].

**Stop signals**

- Dizziness, light-headedness or fainting.
- Headache, flushing or palpitations that persist.
- Chest pain or breathlessness: get emergency care.
- Persistent stomach upset or diarrhoea.
